# AI Watchlist (working)

11-23-26

## Columns
- **Level** (strongest first): **3 - Composite top 30**, in a top-30 table of the AI Composite Shortlist; **2 - Scenario Shortlist**, in the top 5 of a sub-sector's Scenario Shortlist but no composite top-30 table; **1 - Sub-sector Shortlist top 10**, in the top 10 of a sub-sector Shortlist but no Scenario Shortlist; **0 - Added from Candidates**, added by judgment from the data, tools, trust or runtime Candidates files; **Manual add**, added by judgment and not assessed by the pipeline. **Added from physical Candidates**, added by judgment from the physical sub-sectors' Candidates files (cooling, electrical, memory, networking, power, processors, racks, wafers): at least 15% sales growth (TTM or latest quarter), EV/sales of 25 or less, and concentrated on AI. **Re-added**, cut earlier for extreme valuation or weak financial strength and brought back, because both can change and are covered by the Valuation and Financial status reads.
- **Sub-sectors:** from the AI Role pass.
- **Still to run through the passes:** the five Re-added companies (ARM, CRWD, DOCN, NET, TLN) and the 35 Added from physical Candidates have no reads yet and need every pass (News, AI Role, Current Growth, Potential Growth, Valuation, Strength). Nubank (NU) is not in the screener, so its Current growth is empty and its Potential growth, Valuation and Financial status were written without screener figures; it needs Current Growth run from its FMP statements, then Potential Growth and Valuation redone.
- **Potential growth, Valuation, Financial status:** the outputs of the component passes. Financial status is the Strength pass's output: whether the company can keep funding its own growth from the cash its business generates, in good times and in a downturn.

## Sources
- Earlier pipeline outputs: `data/screener/AI Subsectors/` (Candidates, Shortlists, Scenario Shortlists, AI Composite Shortlist 11-18-26).
- Screener data: `data/screener/Large_Actives_with_Metrics.csv` (mid-November 2026).
- News: `data/screener/AI Subsectors/News/` (130 news files, `run_news.py`, `README - News Pass.md`, and `News Summary.md` with News AI and News Sentiment).
- AI Role pass: `data/screener/AI Role/` (Sub-sectors and AI Role).
- Current Growth pass: `data/screener/Current Growth/` (Current growth).
- Potential Growth pass: `data/screener/Potential Growth/` (Potential growth).
- Valuation pass: `data/screener/Valuation/` (Valuation; prices as of the November 20, 2026 close).
- Strength pass: `data/screener/Strength/` (Financial status; FMP statements fetched November 24, 2026, raw files in `raw/`).

| 1 | AAON | AAON, Inc. | 3 - Composite top 30 | cooling | Cooling demand grows as data centers are finished and accelerators get denser: liquid cooling grows with density, and air cooling grows with every new facility. AAON reaches this through BASX, its data center line, which rose 218% to $397.2M in Q3 2026 and is now more than half of revenue, so most of the company's growth now depends on data center cooling. The inputs do not split BASX between liquid and air; from background knowledge rather than the inputs, BASX makes both coolant distribution units and air handlers, which would place it in both parts of the Upside.<br><br>Against trailing sales of $2.3B, the opportunity is large. Q3 revenue of $707.4M, multiplied by four, is about $2.8B a year, and the $1.9B backlog, up 40.5%, equals most of a year of trailing sales. With repeat hyperscaler orders and new customer wins cited, the inference is that orders are still building.<br><br>Current growth shows the company already capturing much of this. Sales rose 71.5% over the trailing year and 84.1% in the latest quarter, up from about 9% a year earlier, and gross margin is below its five-year range, so the growth looks like volume rather than price and is not inflated by unusual margins. A large part of the potential growth is already visible. More appears still ahead, since the trailing figure lags the latest quarter and the backlog covers much of the coming year, though on a larger base the pace would likely be below the current step-up. | The market pays 2.9x EV/sales and 31.4 times earnings. Against the growth, the sales multiple is modest: sales rose 71.5% over the trailing year and 84.1% in the latest quarter, and Q3 revenue annualizes to about $2.8B, so on the current run rate EV/sales is closer to 2.4x, with a $1.9B backlog covering most of a year of trailing sales. The P/E is higher than the sales multiple alone suggests, but it is measured with gross margin 0.3pp below its five-year low, so today's margins do not flatter the multiples; if capacity expansion costs ease, earnings could rise faster than sales and the P/E would look lower on normal margins. That is an inference, and the restated Q1 and Q2 results and the reported material weakness in controls make the earnings figure less reliable than usual, which is a reason to lean on EV/sales here.<br><br>The price path points the same way. The stock is 19.6% lower over the year and still 50.0% below its 52-week high, even after the reported 37.8% weekly jump following Q3, while the growth read says the business has accelerated rather than weakened. This looks like a fall that came with stronger growth, not weaker, and the strongly positive post-Q3 sentiment has so far recovered only part of it. The caveats are real: negative free cash flow, the accounting issues, and a pace that will likely slow on a larger base.<br><br>Overall, little of the expected growth appears to be in the price. A modest sales multiple, margins at a low rather than a peak, and a price still half its high together suggest the market is not paying for the data center growth already visible in the run rate and backlog, though the control weakness and slowing pace on a larger base are reasons it may be discounting it. | AAON's operations generate cash, but not much of it: OCF margin is 7.4% of sales over the last four quarters, and FCF margin is -1.5%, so capital spending of roughly 9% of sales is more than the business currently produces. The company is investing from a thin cash base rather than from a position of strength. SBC is only 0.5% of sales, so the cash that does come in is close to real and stock pay does not flatter it. The five-year record shows that this is not a one-off: FCF margin was 1.1%, 0.8%, 4.7%, -0.3% and then -13.2% in FY2025, so the business has never been strongly self-funding over this period and the latest full year was a large shortfall, which reads as a heavy spending phase (from my own knowledge, AAON has been expanding manufacturing capacity, including for data center cooling). One reassurance is that gross margin is 0.3pp below its five-year low, so today's cash flow is not resting on unusually rich margins; if anything it is being measured near a trough. The gap is not being filled with stock, since the share count fell 0.6%. It appears to have been filled with borrowing: the company carries net debt of 0.4B (USD) and Debt / OCF of 2.6 years. That is within the strong range, but because OCF is thin, a modest fall in operating cash in a downturn would push the ratio up quickly, while capital spending already exceeds cash generation. Overall, AAON currently cannot fund its own reinvestment from its own cash; it has a short history of slim but positive FCF, the shortfall is being covered with debt rather than dilution, and the debt load is manageable today but would offer less room if a downturn cut operating cash while the investment programme was still running. |
| 2 | AMAT | Applied Materials, Inc. | 3 - Composite top 30 | wafers, memory | Wafer and packaging demand tracks accelerator volume with supply tight, so equipment suppliers gain from more wafers and more advanced packaging; in memory, makers are adding capacity in every tier, and equipment suppliers gain from that volume. Applied Materials supplies equipment to both, and chipmaking equipment and services are its whole business, so its growth depends directly on how much its customers build. The AI Role places it where the Upside says demand is growing: management expects semiconductor systems to grow more than 40% in 2026 and advanced-packaging revenue to grow above 70%, and its research partners include the leading foundry and the main memory makers. On sales of $34.4B, it takes a large opportunity to move growth, and this one spans logic, memory and packaging together.<br><br>Current growth shows the upside arriving only recently. Sales grew 21.4% over the trailing year but 53.1% in the latest quarter, up from a slight decline a year earlier, and part of that jump may reflect comparison with a weak quarter. Gross margin is only modestly above its low, so the growth does not look price-driven. FQ4 revenue of $10.41B, multiplied by four, is about $41.6B a year, roughly a fifth above trailing sales, so much of the near-term gain is already set by the current run rate but not yet in the trailing figure. A good part of the potential growth is visible in the latest quarter and still ahead in the trailing numbers; growth beyond that depends on customers continuing to add capacity at the pace management describes. | The market pays 6.9x EV/sales and 23.8 times earnings. Set against the growth, these look moderate rather than stretched: sales grew 21.4% over the trailing year but 53.1% in the latest quarter, and FQ4 revenue annualizes to about $41.6B, roughly a fifth above trailing sales, so on the run rate EV/sales is nearer 5.7x and the P/E would fall further if earnings follow. Management expects semiconductor systems to grow more than 40% in 2026 and advanced packaging above 70%. Gross margin is 3.5pp above its five-year low, so current margins flatter the multiples only slightly; at the low, the P/E would be somewhat higher but not dramatically so.<br><br>The price path is the striking part. The stock is up 31.1% over the year but 59.1% below its 52-week high, which means it rose to roughly three times its year-ago price and then gave most of that back; news reports a fall of about 48% from the June peak by late October and a further drop after a record quarter. Sentiment is mixed, with at least 10 firms cutting targets on valuation while keeping Buy-equivalent ratings, and concerns centered on supply constraints, China and lithography's share of budgets. The growth read, by contrast, shows growth only recently arriving and still building, so this looks closer to a fall in mood than a fall in the business, although the reported market-share loss and the cyclical nature of equipment spending are reasons the market may be cautious.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The multiples on the current run rate leave room for the growth management describes, provided customers keep adding capacity at that pace. | Applied Materials generates strong cash from operations, with an OCF margin of 25.7%, and after capital spending of about 7.7% of sales it keeps an FCF margin of 18.0%, so its reinvestment is comfortably paid for by its own cash. SBC is 2.1% of sales, small relative to FCF, so FCF after SBC is still around 16% of sales and the cash flow is largely real. The five-year history is consistently positive: 17.9%, 28.6%, 27.6%, 20.1% and 18.0%. The latest figure sits at the low end of that range, so today's cash flow is typical rather than an unusual high, and the business has self-funded through every year shown. Gross margin is 3.5pp above its five-year low, a moderate gap that suggests little of current cash flow depends on temporary pricing; even if margins fell back to that low, the company would very likely still cover its current level of capital spending from operating cash. Nothing has needed to fill a gap. The share count is essentially flat at -0.1%, which, given the SBC, suggests the company spends some cash on buybacks to offset dilution. Debt / OCF is 0.7 years, well within the strong range, and the company holds net cash of 2.8B (USD), so its cash on hand would cover all its debt even if cash flow fell. From my own knowledge, semiconductor equipment demand is cyclical, and the five-year record does show FCF margin moving between about 18% and 29%, but it never approached zero. Overall, Applied Materials pays for its reinvestment several times over from operating cash, that cash has been dependable across the years shown and is not propped up by elevated margins, and with net cash and very low debt relative to cash flow it is well placed to keep funding itself through a downturn. |
| 3 | AMZN | Amazon.com, Inc. | 3 - Composite top 30 | runtime, processors, data, tools, trust | Amazon's main AI position is AWS as a cloud provider in runtime, where demand grows as agents are adopted and as more models and accelerators are in use. AWS demand is running ahead of supply: revenue grew 37% to $42.2B in Q2, the backlog is $496B to $578B, and capacity is constrained into 2027, with about $220B of capex this year and two million more Nvidia GPUs planned for 2027-2028. Its Trainium chips sit in the mixed-fleet growth in processors, and the inference is that this shows up mainly through AWS rather than as separate sales. Its positions in data (marketplace data and AWS databases), tools (the marketplace as a destination for shopping agents) and trust (security services and Guardrails, from background knowledge) follow the same agent-driven demand but are small or unsized in the inputs.<br><br>Size limits how much this moves the whole company. Q2 AWS revenue of $42.2B, multiplied by four, is about $169B a year, roughly a fifth of $797.5B in trailing sales, so even fast AWS growth adds only about a fifth of its rate to total growth, and the much larger retail business sets most of the rest. This explains the gap between AWS's 37% and total growth of 15.3% over the trailing year and 12.1% in the latest quarter; the AWS figure better reflects the AI business. Gross margin is meaningfully above its low, which may mean part of the total growth comes from mix or price rather than volume.<br><br>The AI upside is visible inside AWS but only partly in total growth. Much of it appears still ahead, since the backlog is about three times AWS's annual run rate and growth is held back by capacity the planned capex is meant to add, but its effect on total sales is diluted by the size of the rest of the business. | The market pays 3.5x EV/sales and 19.8 times earnings. For a company growing 15.3% over the trailing year and 12.1% in the latest quarter, those multiples are not demanding, but the total growth understates the AI business: AWS grew 37% in Q2 and is only about a fifth of sales, with a backlog of $496B to $578B, roughly three times its annual run rate. The multiples therefore mostly price the larger retail business, and the AWS growth still ahead is only partly reflected in either figure.<br><br>Margins need care. Gross margin is 9.3pp above its five-year low, so the multiples look cheaper than they would at that low. From background knowledge rather than the inputs, much of that rise reflects a mix shift toward AWS, advertising and services, which would make it more durable than a cyclical peak, but if margins fell back the P/E would be noticeably higher. A second point the P/E misses is cash: about $220B of capex this year, with record cash burn reported, means earnings overstate what shareholders currently receive, and rising depreciation from that spending will weigh on future earnings. The moderate P/E may be the market's way of discounting that spending until returns are visible.<br><br>The price path is modest: up 9.1% over the year and 14.1% below its 52-week high, with mixed sentiment that weighs AWS acceleration against spending and return questions. Neither the rise nor the fall is large, so the price does not appear to have moved far ahead of or behind the business.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The multiples do not look stretched for the AWS backlog and acceleration, but margins above their low and heavy capex mean part of the apparent cheapness depends on that spending earning its return. | Amazon's operations generate a great deal of cash, with an OCF margin of 21.1%, but it is spending all of it and more: FCF margin is -5.9%, which implies capital spending of roughly 27% of sales over the last four quarters. This is heavy investment funded from a strong operating base, not operating burn, so it is a choice rather than a necessity. SBC is 2.5% of sales, so FCF after SBC is closer to -8%, meaning the shortfall to owners is somewhat larger than FCF shows. The five-year history makes clear that Amazon has run close to the line for some time: -3.1%, -3.3%, 5.6%, 5.2% and 1.1%. FCF has never been large in this period, and the latest four quarters are the most negative, so the company has consistently put nearly all of its operating cash back into the business. Gross margin is 9.3pp above its five-year low, a sizeable gap; I would read it as a possible sign rather than proof, since from my own knowledge part of the rise may reflect a shift towards higher-margin businesses such as cloud and advertising rather than a temporary condition. If margins did fall back, operating cash would shrink while the capital commitments remained. The gap is not being filled mainly with stock: shares rose 1.2%, which with strong OCF more likely reflects stock compensation. It is being covered by debt and cash on hand. Net debt is 157.7B (USD), and total debt includes Amazon's large lease liabilities, so not all of it is borrowing. Debt / OCF of 1.6 years is strong. Overall, Amazon's operating cash is strong and dependable, but its current investment exceeds it, the history shows very little left over in any year, and the gap is filled with debt that is easily carried today; in a downturn the main protection is that much of this capital spending could be slowed. |
| 4 | ANET | Arista Networks, Inc. | 3 - Composite top 30 | networking | Networking demand grows as more accelerators are deployed and clusters get larger: scale out adds links, scale across grows as computing spreads across sites, and inference adds demand because faster links work around memory limits. Arista sits at all three levels. Its AI fabrics are scale out, the Etherlink SU-144 is an entry into scale up, and an executive says about 30% of its AI business is already scale across. Networking is its whole business, and AI is a growing share, with at least $3.6B of AI revenue expected in 2026, about a quarter of the $13.3B outlook, and AI-fabrics customers grown from a handful to more than 100.<br><br>Against trailing sales of $11.9B, the company's 2027 target of about $18.5B is about 39% above the 2026 outlook, and its $7B from AI fabrics would be more than a third of revenue. These are company targets, not independent estimates.<br><br>Current growth shows the company already capturing much of this. Sales grew 40.7% over the trailing year and 58.5% in the latest quarter, both accelerating, and gross margin near its low gives no sign of price-driven growth. Q3 revenue of $3.66B, multiplied by four, is about $14.6B a year, already above the 2026 outlook, so part of the coming year's growth is set by the current run rate. A substantial part of the potential growth is visible now; the 2027 targets and the newer scale-up and scale-across lines point to further growth still ahead, with scale up barely in current sales. | The market pays 16.6x EV/sales and 44.6 times earnings, high multiples by any measure. The growth behind them is strong: sales rose 40.7% over the trailing year and 58.5% in the latest quarter, and Q3 revenue annualizes to about $14.6B, so on the run rate EV/sales is about 13.5x. On the company's 2027 target of about $18.5B it would be closer to 11x, but that target is the company's own and assumes the newer scale-up and scale-across lines deliver. Even granting that, the multiples ask for the growth to continue at a high rate for several years.<br><br>Margins do not flatter the figures much. Gross margin is only 1.8pp above its five-year low, and news reports it falling to 64%, so the earnings behind the P/E are not inflated by peak margins; if anything, further mix toward AI and large customers could hold margins down rather than lift them.<br><br>The price path suggests much of this is already recognized. The stock is up 37.2% over the year, a large rise accompanied by broadly positive sentiment and Strong Buy ratings, though it is 22.9% below its 52-week high after the October AI sell-off. The fall from the high came with growth still accelerating, which takes some heat out of the price, but coverage repeatedly cites valuation, customer concentration, heavy insider selling and no Ethernet scale-up revenue until trials in 2027.<br><br>Overall, much of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The exceptional, accelerating growth goes some way to justifying the multiples, and the pullback from the high has trimmed them, but at about 13x run-rate sales the price leaves little room for a shortfall against the 2027 targets. | Arista Networks turns a large share of sales into cash, with an OCF margin of 44.1%, and it needs very little of that for equipment: FCF margin is 42.2%, so capital spending is only about 2% of sales. Its reinvestment, most of which (as an inference for a networking company) goes through research and development already taken out of OCF, is easily paid for by its own cash. SBC is 4.3% of sales, so FCF after SBC is still around 38% of sales, and the cash flow is overwhelmingly real. The five-year history is consistently positive: 32.3%, 10.2%, 34.1%, 52.5% and 47.2%. FY2022 stands out as a weak year; from my own knowledge, that was a period when networking companies built up inventory during component shortages, so it looks like a working-capital swing rather than a weakness in the business, and FCF stayed positive even then. Gross margin is 1.8pp above its five-year low, so today's cash flow is not resting on unusually high margins. There is no gap to fill. The share count is almost flat at +0.3%, consistent with modest dilution from stock compensation. Debt / OCF is 0.0 years, meaning debt is negligible, and the company holds net cash of 14.4B (USD), so it is protected even if its cash flow fell sharply. Overall, Arista funds its reinvestment many times over from operating cash, that cash has been positive in every year shown and is not supported by temporarily high margins, and with a large net cash position and essentially no debt it could keep funding itself comfortably through a downturn. |

IT datacom is about 43% of sales and AI about three-quarters of that, so multiplying the two puts AI at roughly a third of sales; IT datacom grew 77% organically in Q3. The rest is diversified, including defense growing 30%.<br><br>On trailing sales of $33.0B, a third is roughly $11B a year of AI business, so its growth moves the total substantially, and record orders of $12.2B against Q3 revenue of $10.23B suggest demand still running ahead of shipments.<br><br>Current growth shows the upside already being captured, but reported figures overstate it. Sales grew 57.6% over the trailing year and 65.1% in the latest quarter, while Q3 organic growth was 37%; the difference is acquisitions, and organic growth better reflects what continues. Gross margin is noticeably above its low, so some growth may come from higher prices. Much of the potential growth is already visible. More appears ahead in AI datacom as orders exceed revenue, while the acquisition boost to reported growth would fade once those deals have been in the base for a full year. | The market pays 5.6x EV/sales and 29.0 times earnings. It is also 22.5% below its 52-week high. A flat price against strong organic growth and rising orders suggests the market has not followed the business up, though margins above their low and acquisition-boosted figures mean the multiples are less cheap than they first appear. | Amphenol's operations generate solid cash, with an OCF margin of 19.6%, and after capital spending of about 4% of sales it keeps an FCF margin of 15.5%, so its reinvestment in plant and equipment is well covered by its own cash. SBC is only 0.5% of sales, so the cash flow is close to real. The five-year history is steady and improving: 10.8%, 14.2%, 17.2%, 14.1% and 19.0%, positive every year, which is the pattern of a self-funding business, and the latest four quarters, at 15.5%, are below FY2025 but within that range. Gross margin is 7.5pp above its five-year low, a meaningful gap that could mean part of today's cash flow rests on unusually favourable demand or mix; if margins fell back, OCF would be lower, but given how small capital spending is relative to OCF, the company would very likely still cover it. The share count rose 1.0% while cash flow was strong and SBC is small, so this more likely reflects option exercises or stock used in acquisitions than funding needs, which is an inference. The main thing to watch is debt. Amphenol carries net debt of 12.6B (USD) and Debt / OCF of 2.8 years, just inside the strong range; from my own knowledge, much of this was taken on for acquisitions, which do not appear in the OCF to FCF gap. In a downturn that cut operating cash, the ratio could move into the moderate range, though the company's consistent cash generation gives it the means to pay the debt down. Overall, Amphenol comfortably pays for its own reinvestment, its cash flow has been dependable for five years even if current margins are somewhat elevated, and the borrowing it carries, mainly from acquisitions, is manageable but leaves less cushion in a downturn than its operating strength alone would suggest. |
| 6 | AVGO | Broadcom Inc. | 3 - Composite top 30 | processors, networking | In processors, accelerator demand grows with the number bought, and mixed fleets spread that growth to custom ASICs alongside GPUs; in networking, demand grows as clusters get larger and every added accelerator has to be linked in. Broadcom sits in both as a designer of custom accelerators (XPUs) and AI networking chips for six major buyers, and AI semiconductors are already the larger part of the business: $16.7B in fiscal Q3 2026, up 221%, more than half of total revenue of $29.6B. The rest is non-AI semiconductors and VMware software, which the inputs do not size separately.<br><br>Against trailing sales of $89.1B, the company's own outlook is very large: AI revenue of $58B in FY2026, about $115B in FY2027 and $230B in FY2028, roughly doubling each year, with the CEO saying supply is secured. These are company targets, not independent estimates. Named programs with OpenAI and Anthropic, and remaining performance obligations of $179.2B, about twice trailing sales, support the inference that the demand is contracted rather than hoped for.<br><br>Current growth shows the upside arriving fast but not yet in the trailing figure. Sales grew 48.7% over the trailing year and 85.5% in the latest quarter, up from about 22% a year earlier. Q3 revenue of $29.6B, multiplied by four, is about $118B a year, about a third above trailing sales, and Q4 AI guidance of $21.7B is higher again. Gross margin is noticeably above its low, so part of the growth may come from price or mix. A meaningful part of the potential growth is visible in the latest quarters, but if the company's outlook holds, most of it is still ahead. | The market pays 16.7x EV/sales and 38.0 times earnings, high multiples on trailing figures. The growth is moving fast, though: sales grew 48.7% over the trailing year and 85.5% in the latest quarter, and Q3 revenue of $29.6B annualizes to about $118B, which would put EV/sales at about 12.6x on the current run rate. The company's outlook of AI revenue roughly doubling each year, to about $115B in FY2027, would lower it much further if it holds, and remaining performance obligations of $179.2B, about twice trailing sales, give that outlook some backing. The P/E is based on reported (GAAP) earnings, and from background knowledge Broadcom's reported earnings carry heavy amortization from the VMware acquisition, so the P/E likely looks higher than underlying earnings would suggest. VMware has been in the base for well over a year, so it no longer distorts EV/sales.<br><br>Gross margin is 7.4pp above its five-year low, so the multiples look somewhat cheaper than they would at normal margins, and news cites memory-heavy XPUs pressuring gross margin, which points to margins moving down rather than up as the AI share rises.<br><br>The price is 19.5% lower over the year and 38.5% below its 52-week high. Part of that looks like mood, with analysts uniformly bullish on the business, but the Nov 20 drop came on a report that Google cut about 1 million chips from its 2027 plans. That is a direct threat to the outlook rather than sentiment alone, and with one customer reported at about half of revenue, concentration is the main way a shortfall would arrive.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price if the company's outlook holds. On the run rate and the outlook the multiples look moderate, but they rest heavily on a few customers delivering on plans that are already being questioned. | Broadcom converts an exceptional share of sales into operating cash, with an OCF margin of 45.6%, and spends very little of it on equipment: FCF margin is 44.2%, so capital spending is only about 1.4% of sales, and its reinvestment is paid for many times over by its own cash. SBC is the one figure that tempers this. At 9.5% of sales it is significant, so FCF after SBC is closer to 35% of sales; that is still a very large amount of real cash, but noticeably less than FCF alone suggests. The five-year history is consistently strong: 48.5%, 49.1%, 49.2%, 37.6% and 42.1%. The FY2024 dip stands out; from my own knowledge, that was the year Broadcom absorbed VMware, so acquisition and integration costs likely explain it rather than any weakening of the business, and FCF has since recovered. Gross margin is 7.4pp above its five-year low, a meaningful gap that could partly reflect favourable conditions or a shift in mix towards software, so I would treat it as a possible sign rather than proof. Even if margins fell back, the gap between OCF and capital spending is so wide that the company would still fund its investment comfortably. The share count rose 1.1%, which with this level of cash flow most likely reflects stock compensation. The main call on cash is debt: Broadcom carries net debt of 35.4B (USD), which from my own knowledge was largely taken on for the VMware acquisition. Data center revenue was 58% of total revenue in Q2, up 107%, so most of the company's growth now depends on it, with PC, embedded and semi-custom chips the rest.<br><br>Against trailing sales of $45.4B, management expects data center revenue to more than double in 2027, a company expectation rather than an estimate here. If a segment that is 58% of sales doubles, it alone would add close to 60 points to total growth, by simple arithmetic. Anchor customers Meta, OpenAI and Anthropic and the MI450 ramp support that expectation.<br><br>Current growth shows the upside already being captured. Sales grew 41.9% over the trailing year and 44.7% in the latest quarter, a steady acceleration from about 32-36% a year earlier. Gross margin is 8.8 points above its five-year low, so part of the growth may come from price or mix, though the low year may have been weak. A substantial part of the potential growth is visible now. If the expected doubling of data center revenue in 2027 holds, more is still ahead, with the new GPU generation, rack systems and server CPUs for agents largely ahead of current sales. | The market pays 15.2x EV/sales and 91.4 times earnings. The implied net margin from the two multiples is about 17%, low for a chip designer with gross margin above its low; from background knowledge, amortization from the Xilinx acquisition weighs heavily on reported (GAAP) earnings, so the P/E looks much higher than underlying earnings support, and EV/sales is the better guide. Xilinx has long been in the base, so it does not distort EV/sales. Gross margin is 8.8pp above its five-year low, so the sales multiple is measured at somewhat flattering margins.<br><br>Against the growth, 15 times sales is a high price. Sales grew 41.9% over the trailing year and 44.7% in the latest quarter, and management expects data center revenue, 58% of sales, to more than double in 2027, which by simple arithmetic would add close to 60 points to total growth. If that happened, EV/sales on 2027 sales would be closer to 9-10x. That is a company expectation, and the current multiple largely assumes it is met; a shortfall in the MI450 ramp or with anchor customers would leave the price exposed.<br><br>The price path suggests much of this was already paid for. The stock is up 97.3% over the year, after topping a $1T market cap in September on euphoric sentiment, and is 35.6% below its 52-week high after falling on a short report about exposure to blacklisted Chinese companies, investor investigations and the Lam supply warning. The growth itself has not weakened, so most of the fall looks like mood, though the short report raises a specific risk the inputs cannot resolve.<br><br>Overall, much of the expected growth appears to be in the price. Even after the fall, about 15 times trailing sales pays for the data center doubling management expects, leaving little room if it arrives later or smaller than planned. | AMD generates cash at an OCF margin of 20.1%, and capital spending of about 4.6% of sales leaves an FCF margin of 15.5%. As a chip designer that relies on outside manufacturers, its equipment needs are light, and its reinvestment, which as an inference runs mostly through research and development already taken out of OCF, is comfortably self-funded. SBC is 4.4% of sales, so FCF after SBC is about 11% of sales; still solid real cash generation, but stock pay takes a meaningful share. The five-year history is positive every year: 19.6%, 13.2%, 4.9%, 9.3% and 19.4%. FY2023 was a weak year; from my own knowledge, that coincided with a downturn in demand for PC chips, and FCF stayed positive even then, which is a useful sign of how it holds up when conditions worsen. Gross margin is 8.8pp above its five-year low, a meaningful gap that could partly reflect a more favourable product mix or demand; if margins fell back, cash flow would be lower, but on its record the company would still very likely generate positive FCF, as it did in FY2023. There is no gap to fill. The share count rose 0.6%, consistent with stock compensation that buybacks are not fully offsetting. Debt / OCF is 1.0 year, well within the strong range, and the company holds net cash of 6.9B (USD), so cash on hand covers all its debt and would protect it in a downturn. Overall, AMD funds its own reinvestment from cash that has been positive in every year shown, including a weak one, although a meaningful share is offset by stock pay and today's margins are somewhat elevated; with net cash and low debt relative to cash flow, it is well placed to keep funding itself through a downturn. |

Part of Q3's growth came from deals such as DMC Power and NSI Industries, and the inputs give no organic total, so underlying growth is somewhat lower than reported. News also cites higher leverage from acquisitions: EV includes that debt in full, while trailing sales include the acquired businesses only since they closed, so EV/sales likely looks somewhat higher than it will once a full year is in the base. From background knowledge, acquisition amortization also weighs on reported (GAAP) earnings, making the P/E look somewhat higher than underlying earnings would.<br><br>Margins pull the other way. Gross margin is 7.9pp above its five-year low, so earnings are measured at margins above normal, and news names margin pressure from inflation, tariffs and integration costs. If margins fell back toward the low, the P/E would be meaningfully higher than 23.5.<br><br>The price is 4.8% lower over the year and 28.6% below its 52-week high, and coverage is moderately positive, with no specific stock moves reported. With growth accelerating, the fall from the high looks like part of the wider retreat in data-center and grid names rather than a change in the business.<br><br>Overall, part of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The multiples are reasonable for a business that has turned from flat to mid-20s growth, partly bought, with a fast-growing data center line; margins above their low and acquisition effects mean the true picture is somewhat less clear than the headline figures. | Hubbell generates cash at an OCF margin of 16.8%, and capital spending of about 2.7% of sales leaves an FCF margin of 14.1%, so its reinvestment in plant and equipment is comfortably covered by its own cash. SBC is 0.4% of sales, so the cash flow is close to real. The five-year history is positive every year and broadly improving: 10.8%, 9.2%, 13.3%, 14.4% and 15.0%, and today's figure is in line with that pattern, which is the record of a dependable, self-funding business. Gross margin is 7.9pp above its five-year low, a meaningful gap that could mean part of today's cash flow rests on favourable pricing or demand; if margins fell back, operating cash would be lower, but with capital spending this modest the company would very likely still cover it. The share count fell 0.6%, which points to modest buybacks. Debt is the main thing to weigh. Debt / OCF is 4.6 years, in the moderate range, and net debt is 4.8B (USD), which looked high relative to the rest of the profile, so I checked the quarterly cash flow statements in the raw file. They show about $2.8B of net debt issued in the June 2026 quarter, with only small buybacks around it, so the borrowing was not used to return cash to shareholders; as an inference, it fits funding an acquisition, which would not appear in the OCF to FCF gap. The company has since begun repaying, with about $0.25B of net repayment in the September quarter. Until more of that debt is paid down, a downturn that cut operating cash would push the ratio towards the weak range. Overall, Hubbell funds its own reinvestment from cash that has been positive for five years and broadly improving, though today's margins are somewhat elevated; the debt it recently took on, apparently for an acquisition, leaves it moderately exposed in a downturn until its consistent cash flow brings that debt back down. |

Some of the AI potential is likely visible in the recent pickup, through AI adoption the CFO cites, but how much cannot be separated from core consumption. The five-year history is positive every year but declined before recovering: 33.3%, 34.7%, 28.5%, 21.0% and 19.1%. From my own knowledge, FY2024 and FY2025 were affected by the large Ansys acquisition and the sale of a business, with related taxes and costs, so the dip looks tied to those events rather than the core business; the latest four quarters are back near 29%. Gross margin is 4.6pp below its five-year low, so current cash flow is not resting on unusually favourable margins; as an inference, the acquisition may have changed the mix. The share count rose 19.6% and net debt is 7.2B (USD), so I checked the quarterly cash flow statements in the raw file. They show about $14.3B of net debt issued in the April and July 2025 quarters, followed by about $4.3B of repayments over the next two quarters, and about $2.0B of new stock in the January 2026 quarter. As an inference, and from my own knowledge, the borrowing and much of the share increase reflect paying for Ansys in cash and stock, and the $2.0B was an investment by Nvidia. Debt / OCF is 3.7 years, in the moderate range. Because the company has already begun repaying and its cash flow is strong and steady, it has the means to bring that down, but in a downturn the debt would weigh more. Overall, Synopsys funds its own reinvestment comfortably from cash that has been positive every year and has recovered after deal-related dips; the main constraint is the debt and dilution from its large acquisition, which leave it moderately exposed in a downturn until the debt is paid down. |
| 130 | U | Unity Software Inc. | Manual add | data | Unity sits in data as a platform whose engine and ad network generate data about games, players and ads, which its Vector ad platform is built on. The Upside says holders can build models and applications on their own data, and Vector is that kind of model, which Wedbush says has become a fully budgeted channel for user-acquisition teams. Unity Spark, an AI game creation platform built with Google, extends into creators. The business splits between the engine and ads, and strategic revenue, which grew 59% on Vector, is the part the AI story centers on; the inputs do not give its share of sales.<br><br>With trailing sales of $2.18B in the source data, the company is small enough that Vector's performance moves its growth substantially.<br><br>Current growth shows a turn from decline. Sales grew 20.9% over the trailing year and 32.2% in the latest quarter, after falling about 8% a year earlier, so part of the trailing figure reflects a weak base. Strategic revenue growing 59%, faster than total revenue, suggests the rest of the business is growing more slowly or shrinking; from background knowledge, Unity has been winding down non-strategic revenue, which holds back total growth. Gross margin is near its low, so growth is not price-driven. Part of the potential growth is visible in the turnaround and in Vector's growth. More appears still ahead as Vector becomes a larger share of sales and Spark brings new creators onto the engine. | The market pays 9.0x EV/sales; there is no P/E because net income is zero or negative, so EV/sales is the only multiple. Gross margin is only 1.2pp above its five-year low, so the sales multiple is not flattered by margins.<br><br>Against the growth, about 9 times sales is a high price. Sales grew 20.9% over the trailing year and 32.2% in the latest quarter, a turn from a decline of about 8% a year earlier, so part of the trailing growth reflects a weak base. Strategic revenue, built around its Vector ad platform, grew 59%, faster than the total, and from background knowledge Unity has been winding down non-strategic revenue, so the business it is keeping is growing faster than reported totals. Even so, a multiple near 9 times sales without reported profits pays for Vector's growth continuing for years and for the company turning that into profits. Unity Spark, its AI game creation platform with Google, is a further source with no figures.<br><br>The price is up 7.6% over the year and 17.5% below its 52-week high, trading mostly around $40-45, with an 11% fall in October despite positive analyst notes. Coverage is mostly positive, with upgrades on the Google partnership. A Motley Fool piece cites early reports of a $25B valuation, which the inference reads as possible takeover interest; if so, some of the price may reflect that rather than the business alone. Executive share sales in August are a caveat.<br><br>Overall, much of the expected growth appears to be in the price. The multiple pays for the turnaround and for Vector continuing to grow quickly, leaving limited room if ad growth slows, while Spark and any AI creation tools are only beginning. | Unity generates cash at an OCF margin of 28.5%, and capital spending of about 0.7% of sales leaves an FCF margin of 27.8%, so its reinvestment, which as an inference for a software company runs mostly through research and development already taken out of OCF, is self-funded on these figures. SBC is 10.8% of sales, so FCF after SBC is around 17% of sales; still solid real cash generation, though stock pay takes a meaningful share. The share count rose 4.1%, and I checked the quarterly cash flow statements in the raw file: they show no buybacks, no new borrowing and only modest stock sales to employees over the last six quarters, so the rise reflects stock compensation that is not being offset, which falls on shareholders as dilution. The five-year history shows steady improvement: -13.8%, -8.4%, 8.2%, 15.1% and 21.8%, with the latest four quarters higher again, so the cash generation has improved every year and is now established for about three years. Gross margin is 1.2pp above its five-year low, so current cash flow does not rest on unusually favourable margins. There is no gap to fill. Debt / OCF is 3.7 years, in the moderate range; the raw file shows no recent borrowing, and from my own knowledge the debt is mainly convertible notes issued in earlier years. The company holds net cash of 0.3B (USD), so its cash exceeds its debt, which limits the risk the ratio would otherwise suggest. Overall, Unity now funds its own reinvestment comfortably from cash that has improved every year, though a meaningful share is offset by stock pay and the dilution that comes with it; its debt is moderate relative to cash flow but covered by cash, leaving it reasonably placed in a downturn. |
